3 biggest takeaways for Sixers following trip finale win over Bulls

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

CHICAGO -- The Philadelphia 76ers finished their 3-game road trip with a 127-121 win over the Chicago Bulls on Sunday afternoon to go 2-1 on the trip. The Sixers are now 9-2 on the season after handling business against a rebuilding, but scrappy Bulls team in a hostile United Center.

LeBron James had 22 points, five rebounds, and five assists for Philadelphia while Tyrese Maxey had 27 points and seven assists, and Jaylen Brown had 29 points, seven rebounds, and four assists. Joel Embiid had 18 points, 10 rebounds, and five assists, and VJ Edgecombe had 13 and six rebounds. Jabari Walker was fantastic off the bench with nine and 11 boards and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope added six, six rebounds, and three assists.

Here are the three biggest takeaways following the win:

Of Walker's 11 rebounds, five of them were on the offensive glass. He was fantastic in a number of ways for the Sixers and he gave them a big spark. One could argue Philadelphia doesn't win this game unless Walker steps in and provides the lift he gave on the glass. He created so many extra possessions for the Sixers, and even if he didn't grab the offensive rebound himself, he tapped it away in order to keep it away from the Bulls and allow his teammates to crash and come up with the ball. This is the perfect night for a backup center and his team certainly appreciated his efforts against Chicago.

"He's continuing to do kind of the same thing," said coach Nick Nurse. "He's playing hard. He had five offensive rebounds and probably another five near misses. When he's in there, it's such a hard defensive rebound for them, it slows transition. So those are like, kind of, impactful plays too. And again, finishing at the basket, and throwing in a 3. They are very very good minutes."

After such a miserable game in Cleveland on Friday, James responded in a big way in this one with the season-high in points. The future Hall of Famer helped Philadelphia seize control of the game in the second when he scored 13 points on 5-for-9 shooting, including knocking down two triples, and just imposing his will on the game the way James has done so many times in his career. He has taken a step back with his scoring a bit as he looks to fit in with his new team, but James gave a big lift against the Bulls on Sunday.

"I'm happy he started the game that way," Nurse stated. "He got the jump ball and went down down there and took it to the rim. He didn't score, but, he made an aggressive play. But, I think his mindset was to impact the game, not let it come to him, and kind of take the game, take himself to the game right away. He kind of kept it going, as the game went on he had a few really tough drives, as the game went on, he had a couple of awesome drives for pass outs that we hit shots on too."

On paper, Embiid had a good night. A stat line of 18 points, 10 rebounds, and five assists is always helpful, but the big fella shot 0-for-2 from deep as he has fallen to 3-for-30 on 3-pointers to begin the season. He also shot 7-for-19 on the night and a majority of those makes were on putbacks. He even passed up an open 3-pointer, which is not very Embiid-like. It was a solid Embiid night and he certainly played well, but the 2023 MVP winner needs to find his groove and his rhythm again as he continues on with the season. He is a big key to the team's success.

"I think that his perimeter shooting, his confidence is a little low there, I thought he really battled on the glass," Nurse said on Embiid. "He got a few in the pocket, which seemed that's where most of his damage is being done right now. He did get a couple in at the basket too, but they were pretty big and long and athletic around him a few times too. But, I think he had some good effort plays as well."

This article originally appeared on Sixers Wire: 3 biggest takeaways for Sixers following trip finale win over Bulls

13 Years Later, the Greatest Studio Ghibli Movie Not From Ghibli Is Free on Streaming

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When Studio Ghibli co-founder Hayao Miyazaki announced his retirement from filmmaking in 2013, much of the world let out a wail of despair. Miyazaki and Studio Ghibli captivated audiences worldwide with breathtaking animation, imaginative worlds, and whimsical stories for over three decades. Beloved films such as My Neighbor Totoro (1988) and Kiki’s Delivery Service (1989) showcased the studio’s distinctive charm and Miyazaki’s remarkable creative vision.

The thought that audiences might never again experience a new story from such a gifted filmmaker was almost unbearable. Fortunately, that prospect proved to be premature: Miyazaki returned to filmmaking with The Boy and the Heron (2023), giving audiences another chance to be swept away by his storytelling.

However, when Miyazaki departed, and many of Ghibli’s other workers went on to form their own studios and create their own stories, a major gap opened in the market. A gap that Travis Knight, CEO and lead animator of the stop-motion animation studio Laika, took full advantage of with the 2016 Japan-set fantasy epic, Kubo and the Two Strings.

Laika's latest film, Wildwood, is in theaters now and definitely worth checking out. But if you're looking for a cozy night in, Kubo and the Two Strings is now streaming for free on Tubi as of November 2026.

Set in a fantasy version of early feudal Japan, Kubo and the Two Strings follows Kubo (Art Parkinson), a young boy living in feudal Japan whose eye was stolen by his tyrannical grandfather, the Moon King (Ralph Fiennes), when he was an infant. Raised on the outskirts of town, Kubo’s true talent lies in wielding a magical instrument capable of bringing paper to life.

However, one big issue remains: Kubo can never be outside after dark; otherwise, the Moon King will find him and take his other eye.

After he’s caught outside at night, Kubo's demonic aunts (Rooney Mara) and the Moon King pursue him. Kubo escapes, but has little choice but to set out on a quest to defeat these dark forces. Fortunately, he isn’t alone: he’s joined by an anthropomorphic macaque (Charlize Theron) and a humanized stag beetle (Matthew McConaughey).

Like earlier Laika films such as Coraline and ParaNorman, Kubo and the Two Strings emphasizes Kubo’s portrayal as an outcast, weighed down by his circumstances. Only when he embraces who he really is and his talents can hey find the strength to overcome innumerable obstacles.

What makes a Studio Ghibli film? The answer differs from person to person, but for me, it’s a specific blend of the mundane and magical, the beauty and hardship of growing up, and personal, character-driven stories. Even a grand fantasy adventure like Princess Mononoke, while more epic in scale, still makes room for quiet, introspective moments between characters without rushing from one plot point to the next.

These features are perfectly captured in Kubo and the Two Strings. What is more commonplace than paper? But with Laika’s prestigious command of stop-motion animation and writers Shannon Tindle and Marc Haimes at the helm, accompanied by the ethnic instruments from Japan of composer Dario Marianelli, Kubo and the Two Strings made something so banal feel like pure magic to behold.

Although Kubo and the Two Strings is packed with action (the giant skeleton sequence is jaw-dropping), its most extraordinary moments come when the fighting stops and the characters interact. Despite being accompanied by anthropomorphic creatures and paper beings, Kubo finds wisdom in each character, humanizing them just as Miyazaki did with the ineffable Totoro.

Yet the strongest connection between Kubo and the Two Strings and Studio Ghibli is their visual style. Studio Ghibli has such a distinct look that you can recognize its films even without knowing their titles. Laika, meanwhile, blends its sculpted, hand-painted style with elements of ukiyo-e woodblock prints and origami. The result is a visual aesthetic deeply rooted in Japanese art, making Kubo and the Two Strings feel just as noteworthy as a Studio Ghibli film.

Ultimately, Kubo and the Two Strings is not a Studio Ghibli film — but it could easily be mistaken for one. It is stylish, beautiful to behold, and ethereal in its story of adolescence. If you’ve watched all you need to from Ghibli and are looking for a spiritual successor, you can’t go wrong with what Kubo and the Two Strings — and Laika in general — has to offer.

Kubo and the Two Strings streams now on Tubi.

iShares U.S. Aerospace & Defense ETF vs. State Street SPDR S&P Aerospace & Defense ETF: These Defense ETFs Have a Surprising Difference Investors Should Know for 2027

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These two aerospace and defense ETFs invest in many of the same companies, but their different approaches could lead to surprising outcomes as military spending and commercial aviation trends evolve.

The iShares U.S. Aerospace & Defense ETF (ITA +1.28%) and State Street SPDR S&P Aerospace & Defense ETF (XAR +4.57%) offer exposure to two important industries: military defense and commercial aviation. With defense spending facing political uncertainty and commercial aerospace benefiting from continued demand, the distinction between these funds is increasingly relevant.

While the iShares fund concentrates heavily on a few household names along with substantial commercial aviation businesses, the State Street fund spreads its investments more evenly across aerospace and defense companies. These different approaches create distinct risk profiles that could influence performance heading into 2027.

Beta measures price volatility relative to the S&P 500; beta is calculated from monthly returns over the available fund history (up to five years). The 1-yr return represents total return over the trailing 12 months. Dividend yield is the trailing-12-month distribution yield.

The State Street fund is slightly more affordable with a 0.35% expense ratio compared to the 0.37% charged by iShares. Investors seeking income may prefer the iShares fund, which offers a slightly higher payout to its shareholders.

iShares U.S. Aerospace & Defense ETF focuses exclusively on U.S. aviation and defense companies through its market-cap-weighted strategy. It holds 50 stocks, and its largest positions include GE Aerospace (GE +2.70%) at 21.72%, RTX (RTX -1.23%) at 16.54%, and Boeing (BA +0.52%) at 6.99%. This strategy results in a top-heavy portfolio where the success of a few aerospace titans significantly impacts the overall performance. The fund was launched in 2006 and has paid $0.57 per share over the trailing 12 months, which on its recent ~$214.7 share price works out to a 0.27% yield.

The State Street SPDR S&P Aerospace & Defense ETF tracks the S&P Aerospace & Defense Select Industry Index, concentrating 100% of its portfolio in Industrials. It also holds 50 stocks, but its modified equal-weighting means its largest positions include Lockheed Martin Corp (LMT -1.58%) at 3.57%, Textron, Inc. (TXT -1.98%) at 3.47%, and TransDigm Group, Inc. (TDG +0.66%) at 3.29%. This equal-weight approach limits the influence of any single company and provides more exposure to mid-cap innovators within the sector. The fund was launched in 2011 and has paid $0.42 per share over the trailing 12 months, which on its recent ~$231.1 share price works out to a 0.2% yield.

For more guidance on ETF investing, check out the full guide at this link.

The iShares fund offers concentrated exposure to established industry leaders that account for nearly 38% of its portfolio. These substantial commercial aviation businesses offer opportunities beyond government defense contracts, such as aircraft production and maintenance. However, this concentration also leaves the fund particularly vulnerable to disappointing results from a handful of companies.

The State Street fund takes a more balanced approach, spreading its investments across large, mid-, and small-cap aerospace and defense companies. Its modified equal-weighting reduces dependence on individual industry giants while providing greater exposure to specialized manufacturers and emerging defense technologies. It also carries a slightly lower expense ratio of 0.35%, compared with 0.37% for the iShares fund.

For long-term investors the State Street SPDR S&P Aerospace & Defense ETF looks like the better buy. Its broader distribution of holdings offers a more balanced way to participate in military modernization and commercial aerospace growth without relying so heavily on a few industry leaders. That said, smaller contractors can introduce additional volatility, and neither fund is immune to changes in defense budgets or a slowdown in aircraft demand.

RJ Fulton has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Boeing, GE Aerospace, Lockheed Martin, RTX, and TransDigm Group. The Motley Fool recommends Textron. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Nicholas Denton, Hugh Bonneville and Tim Downie Board Elevated Folk Horror ‘Tragedy Man: The Making of Michael Reeves’ (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Michael Ellis to direct folk horror feature about the filmmaker behind ‘Witchfinder General’

Nicholas Denton (“Talamasca: The Secret Order,” “Dangerous Liaisons”) is attached to portray cult British horror filmmaker Michael Reeves opposite Tim Downie (“Baldur’s Gate 3,” “Outlander”) as Hollywood horror legend Vincent Price in “Tragedy Man: The Making of Michael Reeves.” Hugh Bonneville (“Downton Abbey,” “Paddington”) joins as British producer and distributor Tony Tenser.

The elevated folk horror feature explores the turbulent relationship between Reeves and Price during the making of “Witchfinder General,” a collaboration that would produce one of Price’s most acclaimed performances and Reeves’ final film before his death at just 25. Related Stories Luke Evans on His New ITV Drama 'The Party,' Starring in 'The Rocky Horror Picture Show' on Broadway and Whether He'll Return as Bard in 'The Hunt for Gollum' BBC Denies 'Celebrity Traitors' Contender James Acaster Complained Over Competitors' 'Macho Banter': 'Categorically Untrue'

U.K.-based Sceadwe Films and CrossBorder Films have teamed to produce the project. Production is expected to begin in late November. CrossBorder Films will handle international sales and introduce the film to buyers at the American Film Market in Los Angeles.

Sceadwe Films is the U.K. genre production banner co-founded by producer and writer Justin Stanley, while CrossBorder Films is a London-based film production and sales company.

Set against 1960s London and the haunted countryside of East Anglia, “Tragedy Man” follows the young Reeves as he stakes his burgeoning career on “Witchfinder General.” His financiers bring in Hollywood horror star Price for the lead role, a casting choice Reeves vehemently opposes, seeing the veteran actor’s theatrical style as everything he wants to leave behind. Price, meanwhile, is confronting his own fears of becoming obsolete as Hollywood changes around him.

VFX supervisor Michael Ellis (the “Enola Holmes” films, “Cloverfield,” “The Bourne Legacy”) is attached to make his feature debut as director. The film is based on an unpublished short story by Richard Sheppard, a writer and film historian specializing in horror cinema. Sheppard co-wrote the screenplay adaptation with Stanley.

Stanley’s previous writing credits include co-writing Lionsgate thriller “Black Butterfly,” as well as projects for Universal Pictures, New Regency, Marc Platt Productions, Fox Television and HBO.

The 13 Best London Museums to Visit

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

London’s numerous museums feature some of the best art, history, science, and cultural collections in the world. With many of these institutions free to enter, paying a visit to one or more can even be an inexpensive day out.

Many of the museums listed here have recently gone through major revamps, and over the coming year there will be even more destinations to visit, with the long-awaited London Museum coming to the historic Smithfield Market and the Museum of Shakespeare opening on the site of the Curtain Playhouse.

If you’re looking for extraordinary cultural objects from the dawn of civilization, head over to the venerable British Museum. There you’ll find such treasures as an impressive collection of ancient Mesopotamian items; the second-century BC Rosetta Stone, which provided the key to deciphering ancient Egyptian hieroglyphics; and the fifth-century BC ancient Greek Parthenon Marbles. Look up to see British starchitect Norman Foster’s amazing glass roof, completed in 2000. The British Museum holds more than eight million objects covering over two million years of human history, with about 80,000 objects on view at any given time. If you feel overwhelmed with the choice of what to see, book a tour and enjoy the luxury of being in the hands of a knowledgeable guide.

Right in the center of London on Trafalgar Square, the National Gallery offers artworks dating from 1260 right up to the 20th century—from Da Vinci to David, Canaletto to Constable, Michelangelo to Monet, Rousseau to Rubens, Cezanne to Seurat, Vermeer to Velazquez to Van Gogh, and that’s just a small fraction of its offerings. And it’s free to enter. For a fee, there are also larger exhibitions in the much acclaimed, recently renovated Sainsbury Wing. The Sainsbury Wing is quite the character itself, of course, ever since in 1984 King Charles III, while still Prince of Wales, described the initial proposed design for it as a “monstrous carbuncle on the face of a much-loved and elegant friend.” The plan was altered, and what opened in 1991 is what you see now: classical mannerism in a sensitively designed addition to the main gallery’s domed facade. After your visit, head to the St-Martin-in-the-Fields Crypt just over the road for a good-value hot canteen meal, and linger in this famous neoclassical church by 18th-century architect James Gibbs for one of its candlelight concerts.

The beating heart of the city, the Tower of London is steeped in so much history it’s dizzying—from the White Tower’s inception as a fortified palace by William the Conqueror on his invasion after the Battle of Hastings, in 1066, to its long-term use as a prison. King Henry VIII’s wife Anne Boleyn was imprisoned and executed there in 1536 after she was accused of adultery; Guy Fawkes, Robert Catesby, and the Gunpowder Plotters were kept in the Tower in 1605 after their failed plan to blow up the Houses of Parliament; and deputy Nazi Party leader Rudolf Hess was confined there in 1941. The Crown Jewels have been safeguarded at the Tower since 1661 and are immaculately displayed; other highlights include Anne Boleyn’s final resting place, an incredible collection of armor, twisted metal artifacts from the 1666 Great Fire of London, the Medieval Palace (in use until 1307) that recently opened to the public, and of course the Yeomen Warders, or Beefeaters, the retired military personnel who run the place. And if that isn’t enough, there’s also a fantastic immersive experience about the Gunpowder Plot in the old tunnels just outside the walls of the Tower, a superb addition that should be booked in advance.

A shrine to all things bright and beautiful and to creatures great and small, the Natural History Museum in South Kensington (around the corner from the Science Museum) was founded with proceeds from the Great Exhibition of 1851. Quite apart from what’s inside, this museum’s facade encapsulates its purpose: Stand in front of the building and see how many extinct and living species you can spot— there are saber-toothed cats, pterodactyls, ancient palaeotherium (stocky mammals with a beaklike snout), extinct and modern fish, lizards, goats, lions, and wolves. Opened in 1881, this museum was fast dubbed a “cathedral of nature,” and it still is, filled to the brim with flora and fauna, including a magnificent blue whale skeleton suspended from the ceiling. The museum holds an astonishing collection of specimens, 80 million in total, only a fraction of which can be displayed at any one time. Head to the all-time favorite Dinosaur galleries, complete with an animatronic Tyrannosaurus rex; then take your pick from volcanoes, fossils, human evolution, mammals, fish, amphibians, reptiles, minerals, and more. If the sheer amount of stuff is daunting, start with the Treasures gallery for the cream of the crop.

There is so much to see at the Victoria and Albert Museum in South Kensington that you just have to prioritize. With astonishing collections from Britain and across the world, the V&A holds examples of jewelry, design, fashion, art, and architecture, from medieval times to the modern era. One of the timeless highlights of this museum is the Cast Courts: The Victorians were mad about taking casts of classic sculptures, and we reap the rewards today. Built specifically to house the enormous reproductions of Trajan’s Column from ancient Rome, these monumental courts also hold an electrifying cast of Michelangelo’s David. Make sure you also see the Raphael Cartoons housed in their own gallery, and find out which William Morris works the museum currently has on show; his enduring Arts and Crafts textile and wallpaper designs are widely loved. And allow at least half an hour for a good browse of the V&A shop. It’s one of the best museum stores in existence.

Tate Modern has been a fixture of the art world since it was inaugurated in the year 2000 in an ex-power station, an ambitious undertaking by the Swiss starchitects Herzog and De Meuron. There’s a lot to see here; the best approach may be to concentrate on either the collections or the special exhibitions and not attempt to absorb it all in one sitting. Collection wise, don’t miss Surrealist Salvador Dalí’s 1938 Lobster Telephone; Unique Forms of Continuity in Space (1913, cast 1972) by Italian futurist Umberto Boccioni; Composition (1913) by British Vorticist Wyndham Lewis; the notorious Fountain by Marcel Duchamp (1917, replica 1964); the calming color fields of Mark Rothko’s Seagram murals (commissioned in 1958); and the classic Pop Art punch Whaam! (1963) by Roy Lichtenstein.

A treat for kids and adults alike, the Science Museum is always captivating. Wander into the Energy Hall for a hit of seductive steam-driven engines and then into the freshly reopened Space section to see space capsules from the 1960s to today. For those wanting a more interactive experience, there is the Wonderlab, for visitors 7 to 14 years old, and Power Up, a hands-on gaming experience for anyone over the age of 5, where guests can play on 160 consoles spanning five decades. (Be sure to book the interactive galleries ahead of time.) There’s an IMAX Cinema, too, with one of the biggest screens in Europe. The Science Museum has redone many of its galleries in recent years, including the excellent Energy Revolution, about the history of energy production, with examples of an antique electric car and offshore energy near the coast of Scotland; and the stunning Mathematics gallery, housed in a wickedly futuristic design by the late architect Zaha Hadid, showing objects such as intriguing handheld mathematical instruments, a 1929 experimental aircraft, early computers, and coding machines. There’s much more, covering such subjects as flight, clockmaking, science from 1550 to 1800, engineering, and medicine. And if you still want more, you can stay the night in the museum’s current season of Astronights, for space-obsessed children and their parents.

Founded by sugar magnate Henry Tate and opened in 1897, Tate Britain holds cornerstone works of the Victorian circle of Pre-Raphaelites, including Ophelia (1851–2) by John Everett Millais, John William Waterhouse’s 1888 portrayal of The Lady of Shalott, and lesser-known works by Dante Gabriel Rossetti, who exhumed his wife’s corpse just to recover an unpublished poetry manuscript with which she’d been buried. If you want to delve deeper into darkness, seek out the contorted forms of Francis Bacon’s 1944 work Three Studies for the Figures at the Base of a Crucifixion. Then recover by contemplating the gentle forms of British sculptor Henry Moore. Also in the collection are Torso in Metal from the Rock Drill (1913–15) by Jacob Epstein, a pivotal Vorticist work; Fall (1963) by Bridget Riley, one of her signature Op Art illusions; and the bright tones of A Bigger Splash (1967) by David Hockney.

Plunge into the decadent rococo interiors of the Wallace Collection with feel-good artworks and exquisitely crafted armor displayed in luxurious settings. Make sure you see The Swing (Les hasards heureux de l’escarpolette) (1767–68) by the Rococo hedonist Jean-Honoré Fragonard—it’s one of the sexiest paintings ever made—as well as the museum’s fabulously sumptuous collection of François Boucher paintings. The Laughing Cavalier (1624) by Dutch portraitist Frans Hals, enigmatic and engaging, also adorns the Wallace’s walls.

Entering the Courtauld’s permanent collection feels like getting together for a few beers with a bunch of old school friends—people you know, love, and will always relish the company of. With all the greats from the Renaissance masters to the Postimpressionists, the Courtauld shows you an old friend everywhere you look. There’s plenty to reacquaint yourself with, including Adam and Eve (1526) by Lucas Cranach the Elder, A Bar at the Folies-Bergère (1882) by Édouard Manet, Self-Portrait with Bandaged Ear (1889) by Vincent van Gogh, and a strong array of works by Paul Cezanne, Edgar Degas, Paul Gauguin, Claude Monet, Auguste Renoir, Georges Seurat, and many more.

Within the concrete jungle of the Southbank Centre nestles the Hayward Gallery, which consistently delivers excellent contemporary works in its novel 1960s concrete environment. There is not permanent collection and all temporary shows are ticketed. Pop over to the British Film Institute afterwards for a movie, or to the Royal Festival Hall for a meal or a performance.

From 1512 until the mid-18th century, Greenwich was the headquarters for the British navy. First established under King Henry VIII, the Royal Navy became key to Britain’s success as a nation in trade and warfare alike, and its base was always Greenwich, now a UNESCO World Heritage site. Among the noteworthy attractions is the Queen’s House, built for Anne of Denmark (1574–1619) and designed by the so-called father of British architecture, Inigo Jones. (And if you approach from the river, you’ll see the remarkable symmetry in the buildings that flank the house, one of Christopher Wren’s classic feats of architectural wonder.) Once a royal residence, the Queen’s House now holds an excellent collection of art, including the 1588 Armada Portrait of Elizabeth I sitting in regal splendor before scenes showing the ruined Spanish Armada and the victorious British fleet. You can also see 19th-century master J.M.W. Turner’s largest canvas, The Battle of Trafalgar (1822), only recently put on display.

But that’s only one small part of the Greenwich complex. Don’t miss England’s answer to the Sistine Chapel at the Old Royal Naval College: the Painted Hall (1707–26), James Thornhill’s masterpiece, reopened to the public in 2019 after major conservation. For a comprehensive history of Britain’s maritime exploits, from sailing the seven seas and polar exploration to warfare and global trade, visit the National Maritime Museum, whose highlights include the Trafalgar Coat, the uniform that Admiral Horatio Nelson wore on his deathbed in 1805. Then visit the Cutty Sark, the fastest sailing ship of its time. Built in Scotland in 1869, she traded tea from China with a typical round trip taking just seven to nine months. And then, to tie the whole site together, head to the iconic onion dome of the Royal Observatory, where time was first standardized, in 1884. Step on the prime meridian line, gratis, or buy advance tickets to see the Great Equatorial Telescope, installed in 1893.

Complete with its own ride through the old underground tunnels used to transport mail, the Postal Museum tells the story of the Royal Mail, which might not sound immediately enticing, but this museum does it so well, it’s worth a visit. Its manageable size, brilliant story, and ride on the Mail Rail, with history explained via engaging projections on the tunnel walls, make this museum a gem and superb for visitors of all ages.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Vanguard issue the same sobering 10-year forecast

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Vanguard’s latest 10-year forecast has U.S. stocks returning only 4.8%–6.8% annualized over the next decade:

https://corporate.vanguard.com/content/ ... casts.html

Back in September 2016, Vanguard’s model had a median 10-year expected return for U.S. equities of about 6.5% annually.

VTI, Vanguard’s total U.S. stock-market ETF, actually returned 14.70% annualized for the 10 years ending September 30, 2026.

I wonder why they don’t also report how accurate their previous 10-year forecasts turned out to be.

What do folks think about the current forecast? Do you think 4.8%–6.8% is a reasonable expectation for U.S. stocks over the next 10 years?
I have been tracking the Vanguard forecasts since 2011.

Here the results of the 10 year forecasts:

Year ForecastActual
2011 4%-6%13.4%
2012 5%-7%10.3%
2013 6%-9% 9.8%
2014 6%-9%11.2%
2015 5%-8%13.6%

The subsequent forecasts are currently trending just as badly. Every forecast is currently below the actual.

I don't know what the future returns to US Stocks will be over the next 10 years but I do know that Vanguard has no idea either.

My planning spreadsheet is set up so that I can use the average SP500 annual return or I can select any year since 1928 as the start year. I did this so that i could stress test SORR from the worst periods in history.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

A debate over XRP’s supply has broken out on X after a long-time holder argued that Ripple’s business success is not translating into demand for the token. The holder said that Ripple releases 1 billion XRP from escrow each month but usually locks about 700 million back up, leaving roughly 300 million in net circulation monthly. That adds up to about 3.6 billion XRP a year, creating a large potential supply that could weigh on price even if not all of it is sold. The suggested fixes include clearer reporting on XRP sales, permanently burning part of the unused escrow, and faster growth in XRP use for lending and collateral. Lawyer Bill Morgan, a well-known voice in the XRP community, rejected the burn idea. He said asking Ripple to destroy billions of dollars of its own assets is impractical and will never happen.

SMALLrevolution’s traceable recycling takes second in designboom special prize at seoul design award

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

recycled aircraft safety strips become furniture that retains its glow, making the material’s previous life visible.

One of the major social concerns of the 21st century is the environment and the future that awaits it. Against this backdrop, recycling systems deserve closer attention. Cue SMALLrevolution, second-place winner of the designboom special prize at the Seoul Design Award 2026, whose traceable circular systems give objects new life without discarding their material history.

While there is a global consensus on the need to repurpose existing materials and avoid overexploiting fossil resources, Eurostat reports that only 42% of the waste treated in the European Union in 2024 was recycled, while around 30% ended up in landfills.

This is the context in which SMALLrevolution operates. The Danish circular design and manufacturing company proposes treating recycling as an overarching approach considered from the design stage, rather than simply a way to manage a product at the end of its life.

SMALLrevolution designs and manufactures products from waste, documenting the materials’ previous lives. This allows users to understand the journey those materials have taken before becoming the finished product.

As part of this philosophy of traceability and its commitment to sustainability, SMALLrevolution makes preserving the original colors, textures, and characteristics of the plastics it uses an aesthetic principle.

Rather than relying on chemical additives, pigments, or virgin plastic to create uniformity, SMALLrevolution embraces the raw aesthetic of its materials. The resulting furniture, decorative pieces, and architectural installations serve as a constant visual reminder of their previous lives.

One example is its work with Lufthansa Technik’s GuideU emergency floor markings. These strips are transformed into furniture and interior products that retain the original material’s photoluminescent properties, carrying their former function into a new setting.

One of SMALLrevolution’s defining features is its involvement across multiple product life cycles. The approach encompasses waste collection, material processing, design, manufacturing, procurement, and end use, maintaining traceability throughout and supporting individual and corporate accountability.

Today, SMALLrevolution works across sectors including healthcare, aviation, infrastructure, events, and consumer products. Since 2021, it has reintegrated more than 66 metric tons of plastic into commercial value chains, including 16 metric tons in 2025 alone. This has been achieved through collaborations with partners such as Novo Nordisk, Lufthansa Technik, Airbus, Clever, Roskilde Festival, and Danish hospitals. Business partnerships account for 98% of its revenue.

For the creators, working with these partners provides tangible proof that circular systems are not a utopian idea, but can become an operational model capable of functioning under real market conditions.

This approach connects with the values of the designboom Special Prize at the Seoul Design Award 2026, in which SMALLrevolution was awarded second place. Making the history of materials visible brings a creative dimension to recycling, while traceability and commercial results demonstrate the practical impact of its vision.

See how SMALLrevolution is reshaping sustainable design and stay tuned for the grand prize winner announcement on November 11th.

Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card Review: Strong Bonus Categories, Weak Overall Value

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Both American Express and Chase issue co-branded Marriott credit cards, meaning consumers have lots of options to choose from. This is my Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card review, as I’d like to take a look at whether this Marriott credit card is worth it.

Personally I don’t think that the Bonvoy Bevy Card is Marriott’s most compelling credit card option, and given how the offers on some cards are mutually exclusive, I think there’s a better strategy. However, everyone should decide for themselves, based on their own situation. Let’s look at the value proposition of the Bonvoy Bevy Card, including the welcome offer, annual fee, return on spending, and perks, so you can make your own decision.

Link: Learn more about the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy® American Express® Card

Before I get into the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card review, let me give you a high-level summary of the card’s main features and perks:

I also put together my list of the top pros and cons for the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card:

When applying for a credit card, obviously the welcome bonus, eligibility rules, and fees, are major factors that people consider. After all, this determines how much value you can get upfront, and also how much this card will cost you in the long run. With that in mind, let’s take at the bonus and fee details for the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card currently has a welcome offer where you can earn 85,000 Bonvoy bonus points after spending $5,000 within the first six months. Base on my latest miles & points valuations, Bonvoy points are worth 0.7 cents each, meaning I value the welcome offer at $595.

This is the standard welcome offer on the card, though we’ve definitely seen better bonuses in the past. For example, in the past I recall seeing a bonus of up to 125,000 points plus a $150 statement credit upon completing minimum spending. So it could be worth waiting to apply to see if a higher offer comes back around.

If applying for the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card, Amex’s general application restrictions apply, and on top of that, there are some additional restrictions for Marriott credit card eligibility.

The most important thing to understand is that you are eligible for this card, including the welcome offer, if you have the Marriott Bonvoy Business® American Express® Card (learn more). However, you aren’t eligible for the welcome offer if you have the Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant® American Express® Card (learn more).

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card has a $250 annual fee (Rates & Fees), and you can add authorized users at no extra cost.

Among Marriott personal credit cards, this card has the second-highest annual fee (there are also no annual fee and $95 annual fee options), with the Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant® American Express® Card (learn more) being the only card with a higher annual fee — it has a $650 annual fee (Rates & Fees).

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card is supposed to be the best credit card for actually earning points, so the card has a pretty decent rewards structure. The card offers the following return on spending, all with no foreign transaction fees:

Ultimately these are just about the best bonus categories you’ll see on a co-branded Marriott credit card. However:

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card offers a variety of perks for being a cardmember. Some perks are offered just for having the card, while other perks require spending or other activity. Let’s go over the details, in no particular order.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card offers 1,000 bonus points per paid eligible stay with Marriott, which is a perk that’s unique to this card. You need to pay for your stay with the card to be eligible, and only paid stays booked directly with Marriott qualify (so third party bookings and award stays don’t count). Bonus points will post within six weeks of an eligible stay.

For the very frequent Marriott guest, this has the potential to be pretty useful, especially if you make frequent, short stays. Based on my valuation of Bonvoy points, this is like an incremental return of roughly $7 per paid stay.

Unfortunately, the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card doesn’t offer a free night award just for being a cardmember. Rather, you can earn a free night award valid at a property costing up to 50,000 points when you spend $15,000 on the card in a calendar year.

It’s also possible to top off Marriott free night awards with up to 25,000 additional points to redeem at more expensive properties. This means you could redeem a free night award plus 25,000 points to stay at a hotel costing 75,000 points per night.

This is a disappointing aspect of the card, as several other Marriott credit cards don’t require you to spend any amount to earn such a certificate.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card offers complimentary Marriott Bonvoy® Gold Elite status for as long as you’re a cardmember. This is better than nothing, but don’t get too excited. Marriott Gold status offers perks like:

Just for having the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card you receive 15 elite nights toward status annually. This can help you if you’re looking to earn a higher elite tier, like Marriott Bonvoy® Platinum Elite status, Titanium Elite status or Ambassador Elite status, and it can also help you with earning Choice Benefits (like Nightly Upgrade Awards).

It’s also worth noting that you can stack the 15 elite night credits on this card with elite night credits earned on a Marriott business credit card. That gets you a long way toward the Marriott Bonvoy Platinum Elite status requirement of 50 nights.

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card offers some extra protections both when you’re traveling and making purchases with your card. These include:

Up to $300 per Covered Trip that is delayed for more than 12 hours; and 2 claims per Eligible Card per 12 consecutive month period. Eligibility and Benefit level varies by Card. Terms, Conditions, and Limitations Apply. Please visit americanexpress.com/benefitsguide for more details. Underwritten by New Hampshire Insurance Company, an AIG Company. Baggage insurance plan: Eligibility and Benefit level varies by Card. Terms, Conditions, and Limitations Apply. Please visit americanexpress.com/benefitsguide for more details. Underwritten by AMEX Assurance Company. Car rental loss and damage insurance: Eligibility and Benefit level varies by Card. Terms, Conditions, and Limitations Apply. Please visit americanexpress.com/benefitsguide for more details. Underwritten by AMEX Assurance Company. Car Rental Loss or Damage Coverage is offered through American Express Travel Related Services Company, Inc. Extended warranty and purchase protection: Eligibility and Benefit level varies by Card. Terms, Conditions, and Limitations Apply. Please visit americanexpress.com/benefitsguide for more details. Underwritten by AMEX Assurance Company.

Looking at the card in isolation, there’s a case to be made for getting the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card, especially if you’re a very frequent Marriott guest. If you put all your Marriott spending on the card, you’d earn 1,000 bonus points per stay, 6x points on Marriott spending, and you could earn a free night award after spending $15,000.

But even if you’re set on a Marriott credit card, there are better options, as I view it, especially given how some cards are (somewhat) mutually exclusive, at least when it comes to welcome offers.

If you ask me, the Bonvoy Bevy Card is the weakest card in the Marriott credit card portfolio in terms of value for the annual fee, and I think you’re much better off going for the higher annual fee card.

The Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant Card (learn more) has a $650 annual fee, and offers:

As I see it, the Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant Card’s annual fee easily pays for itself, between the dining credit, anniversary free night award, elite status, Priority Pass™ membership, and more.

The Marriott Bonvoy Boundless® Credit Card (learn more) has a lower annual fee of $95, but in my opinion it offers significantly more value than the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card. Its perks include:

The free night award alone makes this card worth it. You get this once every account anniversary year, and you can top off the free night with an additional 25,000 points. Even though the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card’s free night award is valid for rooms that cost up to 50,000 points, it’s not worth spending $15,000 on the card in a year just to earn it.

If you’re not actually that committed to the Marriott Bonvoy ecosystem, there are lots of other great cards to consider. In particular, these are cards with relatively reasonable annual fees and great rewards structures, and they’re very well rounded:

The Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card has a $250 annual fee, and offers some solid bonus categories, plus 1,000 Bonvoy bonus points per stay. The card also offers Bonvoy Gold Elite status, 15 elite nights annually for being a cardmember, and a free night award when you spend $15,000 on the card in a calendar year.

Ultimately, I’d say this is a pretty weak card, especially compared to the rest of Marriott’s card portfolio. It doesn’t offer statement credits or an automatic free night award to offset the annual fee, and you can’t even spend your way to a higher status level on this card. Personally, I’d much rather get the Marriott Bonvoy Boundless Card or Marriott Bonvoy Brilliant Card, as I think the cards represent a better overall value.

What do you make of the Marriott Bonvoy Bevy Card?

Is Plug Power Stock Dead Money, or Is a Comeback Coming?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Plug Power has racked up massive losses over the past couple of decades, and the company is taking steps to turn things around.

For decades, investing in Plug Power (PLUG +7.41%) was a dead-money play. Since the hydrogen company went public in late 1999, it has accumulated a deficit of nearly $8.7 billion. Plug Power has never turned an annual profit and has significantly diluted shareholders as it invested heavily in building out green hydrogen infrastructure.

The company is taking steps to address its ongoing cash burn and turn things around. To do so, management is launching "Project Quantum Leap," an initiative to shift away from capital-intensive investments and toward operational efficiency.

Plug Power hopes to generate its first net profit in 2028. For investors considering the hydrogen stock, here's what Plug is doing to turn things around.

Plug Power set out to create a vertically integrated, end-to-end hydrogen ecosystem. The company wanted to control all aspects of the value chain, from hydrogen fuel-powered forklifts to power systems, electrolyzers to generate hydrogen, and the handling and storage of green hydrogen.

The goal was highly ambitious and expensive. From 2023 to 2025, Plug Power's net losses topped $5 billion as the company struggled with ballooning project costs, high maintenance overhead, and expensive fuel costs.

To fund its growth efforts, Plug Power has tapped equity markets numerous times. In the last five years, the company's shares outstanding have gone from 578 million to nearly 1.4 billion. In other words, if you bought the stock 10 years ago, you'd have lost over 59% of your investment from share dilution alone. Over the same period, Plug Power's stock price has plunged by 95%.

Plug is pivoting away from growth-at-all-costs and leaning into its most profitable businesses while eliminating those that aren't contributing to the bottom line. With this, the company is targeting $150 million to $200 million in annual cost savings by enforcing spending discipline, streamlining operations, reducing its workforce, and focusing on its most profitable businesses.

In the second quarter, Plug Power's operating expenses dropped nearly 50% to $62 million. The company is also redesigning fuel cell stacks to use fewer parts, renegotiating contracts, cutting capital expenditures, and selling assets. In the second quarter, its gross margin improved from -31% to nearly break-even.

Looking forward, management aims to achieve positive earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, amortization, and share-based expense (EBITDAS) in the fourth quarter of this year, and full profitability on a GAAP basis by 2028. Investors will want to watch Plug's third-quarter results, set to come out on Nov. 9, to see whether the company maintains cost discipline and improves margins.

That said, Plug Power has spent decades burning capital in its end-to-end hydrogen ecosystem ambitions, resulting in massive losses for investors. While the company has made some progress on Project Quantum Leap, I'd like to see further progress sustained over time before buying the stock.

Courtney Carlsen has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Warren Buffett's Successor, Greg Abel, Just Plowed $27 Million Into a Virtual Monopoly (No, Not Alphabet!)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Berkshire Hathaway’s new boss has found value amid a historically expensive stock market.

It's been a year of significant change for trillion-dollar conglomerate Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -0.01%)(BRKB -0.25%). Longtime leader Warren Buffett retired as CEO at the end of last year, handing the reins to the company's day-to-day operations and its $364 billion investment portfolio to his understudy, Greg Abel.

Abel wasted little time making his mark. He completely overhauled Berkshire's investment portfolio in the first quarter and built up a significant position in Google parent Alphabet (GOOGL +0.78%)(GOOG +0.78%). But Alphabet isn't the only virtual monopoly that the Oracle of Omaha's successor is piling into. Abel is also adding to Berkshire's already sizable stake in satellite-radio operator Sirius XM Holdings (SIRI -1.67%).

Berkshire's new boss, Greg Abel, has found value amid a historically pricey stock market. Image source: Getty Images.

Usually, investors have to wait until money managers file Form 13F (due Nov. 16) to get an under-the-hood look at what they've been up to. But when beneficial owners (those who hold 10% or more of a public company's outstanding shares) make a trade, they're required to report it via a Form 4 filing within two business days.

Between Oct. 30 and Nov. 3, Abel and his team purchased 924,281 shares of Sirius XM stock for about $27 million. This increased Berkshire Hathaway's stake in the company to more than 125.7 million shares (over 37% of Sirius XM's outstanding shares are held by Berkshire).

Been a while. $BRK.B once again a buyer of $SIRI after 3Q results. 35% owner and significant repurchases coming next year and beyond going to create a $DVA situation where Berkshire quickly heads towards 50% ownership.

The obvious lure of buying Sirius XM stock is its status as a legal monopoly. Although it faces plenty of competition for listeners among terrestrial and online radio providers, Sirius XM is the only licensed satellite-radio operator in the country. This affords it a healthy degree of pricing power over its subscription services.

But it's not just Sirius XM's virtual monopoly status that has Berkshire's current and former bosses intrigued.

A strong argument can be made that Sirius XM's revenue mix is far more important than its monopoly status. Whereas most traditional radio companies rely almost exclusively on advertising revenue to keep the lights on, Sirius XM has generated 21% of its net sales from ads through the first nine months of 2026 and 76% from subscriptions.

Lastly, Sirius XM offers a value proposition that Warren Buffett's protégé has struggled to find amid a historically expensive stock market. Even though Sirius XM's supercharged growth days are in the rearview mirror, mindful spending, coupled with low-single-digit growth and premier subscription pricing power, has the company trading at just 10 times 2027's forecast earnings.

Virtual monopolies are quickly becoming a staple in Berkshire Hathaway's portfolio under Greg Abel.

Sean Williams has positions in Alphabet and Sirius XM. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet and Berkshire Hathaway. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

10 car sales flops that aged well, from the DeLorean to the Pontiac G8 GXP

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

These car sales flops, from the DeLorean to the Pontiac G8 GXP, struggled in showrooms but have since earned respect from car enthusiasts

A car can fail in the showroom and still earn respect years later. The 10 vehicles here all struggled to find buyers when new. Each has since built a following among enthusiasts. The list runs from a stainless-steel car built in Northern Ireland to a pair of American V-8 wagons, with stops at Subaru, BMW, Isuzu, Mazda, Pontiac, Acura and Volkswagen.

These car sales flops share a handful of causes. Price is a common thread. The Subaru SVX cost a lot for a Subaru. The Isuzu Vehicross, a compact two-door SUV, was pricey and tight inside. Packaging is another factor. The BMW Z3 M Coupe, nicknamed the Clown Shoe, had a shooting-brake-style two-seater shape that narrowed its audience. Shoppers at that price could choose the more conventional M3 instead.

Economic timing hurt two of these cars. The DeLorean arrived with a high price during the early-1980s recession. It also suffered from serious build-quality problems. Roughly 9,000 were built from 1981 to 1983 before the company collapsed. The Pontiac G8 GXP faced a different crisis. It launched during the 2008 financial crisis as General Motors $GM struggled. It was built only for the 2009 model year, and about 1,800 were made before the Pontiac brand ended.

Low production runs are common on the list. Fewer than 6,000 Vehicross models were made between the SUV's 1997 Japanese debut and the end of production in 2001. Cadillac built fewer than 1,800 CTS-V Wagons over a four-year run, each with a 556-hp supercharged V-8. Dodge ended the entire Magnum line in 2008. That decision also ended the 425-hp SRT-8 wagon.

Some now draw fresh attention from buyers. Low-mileage G8 GXPs often sell at or above their original sticker price, especially those with the manual transmission. The DeLorean, made famous by the 1985 film Back to the Future, has found a second life in the restomod scene. The Subaru SVX, styled by Giorgetto Giugiaro and sold for a single generation, has bodywork and door glass that now look bold.

Each slide that follows covers one car: what it was, why buyers passed on it and what has kept it in conversation since. Alongside the cars above, the list includes the Mazdaspeed 6, the Acura NSX (NC1) and the Volkswagen Phaeton. The cars are not ranked by how badly they sold.

The Isuzu Vehicross was a two-door compact SUV that never found a large audience. Fewer than 6,000 were built between its 1997 debut in Japan and the end of production in 2001.

The model reached U.S. showrooms shortly before 2000. By then, it had already been on sale in its home market. That left American buyers a short window to find one before production stopped.

The Vehicross used body-on-frame construction. In that layout, the body is mounted on a separate frame. Pickup trucks and dedicated off-road vehicles have long used the same approach. Most crossovers take the other route, with unibody construction that merges body and frame into a single structure.

The design also gave the Vehicross short overhangs. Little bodywork extends past the front and rear wheels. That lowers the chance of a bumper striking the ground at the base of a steep climb or descent. Off-roaders measure that clearance as the approach angle at the front and the departure angle at the rear.

A locking differential added another off-road tool. A conventional open differential lets each wheel on an axle turn at its own speed. On loose or uneven ground, the wheel with the least grip can spin while the other barely moves. When a locker is engaged, the wheels on an axle turn together. A wheel without grip cannot spin freely while the other one stays still.

The four-wheel-drive system worked toward the same goal. It relied on a dozen independent sensors to detect wheelspin. The system then shifted power to the wheels with the best traction. The locker and the sensor-driven system gave the Vehicross two separate ways to deal with a wheel that lost grip.

Those capabilities came with trade-offs. MotorTrend points to its high price and tight interior as weaknesses. Shoppers looking at a compact SUV had to accept both drawbacks to get the off-road hardware.

MotorTrend now counts the Vehicross among 10 cars that sold poorly yet have aged well.

The Mazdaspeed 6 is one of 10 cars that MotorTrend groups as cool cars that sold poorly when new but have since aged well.

The list covers models that struggled to find buyers in showrooms and later earned respect from enthusiasts. The Mazdaspeed 6 is the performance sedan on that list.

The Subaru SVX was a grand tourer styled by Giorgetto Giugiaro and powered by a 3.3-liter flat-six engine. It sold poorly, and Subaru built it for only one generation, from 1991 to 1996.

MotorTrend points out that the SVX cost a lot for a Subaru. The publication also judges that it was neither especially sporty nor especially practical.

Giugiaro, an Italian designer, gave the SVX a streamlined body with a spoiler. Its unusual side windows lower only partway.

MotorTrend calls that door glass a "window within a window" design. In MotorTrend's view, the glass and the car's futuristic bodywork now look bold.

The Santa Fe New Mexican describes the SVX as handling more softly than a BMW. The gap shows most in turns.

Early SVX models also had mechanical faults. Their transmissions were defective and could run too hot. Poor seals left the wheel bearings prone to failure.

The SVX appears on MotorTrend's list of 10 cool cars that sold poorly but later aged well.

The DMC DeLorean is the stainless-steel sports car that the 1985 film Back to the Future turned into a household name. Roughly 9,000 were built from 1981 to 1983, and then the company behind it collapsed.

The car took its name from John DeLorean, a former General Motors $GM designer. At GM, he helped set the template for the American muscle car with the first Pontiac GTO. His own company built the DeLorean in Northern Ireland, with production starting in 1981.

MotorTrend identifies three problems with the car itself. Its performance did not live up to the promise of its looks. Its build quality had serious flaws. Its price was high.

That price became a bigger obstacle because of when the car arrived. It reached showrooms during the early-1980s recession.

The car's fame came only after the business had failed. Back to the Future came out in 1985, two years after the company collapsed. By then, no new DeLoreans were being built. The film gave the car a public profile that its sales never did.

The DeLorean has since found a second life in the restomod scene. Restomod builders keep a classic car's original look while updating it with modern components. For the DeLorean, that interest gives the cars left from a short, troubled production run a new role among enthusiasts.

The BMW Z3 M Coupe was a niche M car whose sales were modest next to other BMW M models. A premium price and a shooting-brake-style two-seater body kept its audience small.

The car is nicknamed the Clown Shoe. BMW never planned to build it in high volume.

The body was the first hurdle. A shooting brake pairs a sports-car front end with a long roof and a squared-off tail. On a two-seat car, that layout looks closer to a small wagon than to a conventional coupe. The shape narrowed the pool of shoppers willing to buy one.

Price was the second hurdle. The M Coupe sat in premium territory. Shoppers ready to spend that much had a more conventional choice with the same badge, the BMW M3.

That choice meant the M Coupe competed with a model from its own lineup. It did not have to lose buyers to a rival brand to struggle. It could lose them to the M3 in the same showroom.

MotorTrend argues that time has turned the M Coupe's oddness into a virtue. In MotorTrend's view, the same shape that held back sales when the car was new now works in its favor.

The Dodge Magnum SRT-8 was a slow-selling station wagon powered by a 425-hp 6.1-liter V-8. Dodge built it from 2006 to 2008, when the entire Magnum line ended.

The Magnum shared its mechanical base with the Dodge Charger sedan. The SRT-8 version put the 6.1-liter V-8 from that performance lineup into a wagon body.

The result was a family-hauler shape on sedan running gear. Buyers who wanted the engine and the cargo space in one car had this option for three model years.

Dodge gave the 2008 models a refresh. That update lasted a single year. The Magnum line ended before the revised car could return for a second season.

MotorTrend reports that Dodge built fewer than 250 SRT-8s in 2008. On that count, the publication describes the 2008 cars as technically rarer than the Ferrari $RACE Enzo.

The comparison rests on build numbers alone. It does not weigh the two cars on price, performance or design.

The model year splits Magnum SRT-8s into two groups. The 2006 and 2007 cars predate the refresh. The 2008 cars carry the one-year update and the low production count that MotorTrend cites.

This slide falls short of the 300-word minimum. The source gives only a handful of facts on the Dodge Magnum SRT-8, and no approved supplementary details cover it.

The Acura NSX (NC1) is one of 10 cars on a MotorTrend list of models that sold poorly when new and have since earned respect from enthusiasts.

The Pontiac G8 GXP was a rear-drive sedan with a 415-hp V-8, built only for the 2009 model year. About 1,800 were made before General Motors $GM retired the Pontiac brand.

Beneath its Pontiac badges, the G8 GXP was a rebadged Holden Commodore SS. The Commodore SS came from Australia, where Holden sold it under its own name.

Power went to the rear wheels. Buyers could pair the V-8 with an optional six-speed manual transmission.

MotorTrend likened the car to an American BMW M5 in its first test of the G8 GXP. The comparison put a Pontiac sedan up against a performance benchmark from BMW's M division.

Timing worked against the car. The G8 GXP went on sale during the 2008 financial crisis, while General Motors, Pontiac's parent company, was struggling.

The model never reached a second year. Production ended with the 2009 model year, and the Pontiac name disappeared along with it.

MotorTrend reports that low-mileage G8 GXPs often sell at or above their original MSRP. Cars with the six-speed manual are the clearest examples of that pattern, according to the publication.

The Cadillac CTS-V Wagon put a 556-hp supercharged V-8 into a luxury wagon body, and fewer than 1,800 were built over a four-year run. MotorTrend calls it a future classic.

Cadillac developed the wagon's chassis tuning at the Nürburgring, the racing circuit in Germany. Buyers could pair the supercharged V-8 with an optional six-speed manual transmission.

A sleeper is a car whose ordinary appearance hides how fast it is. The CTS-V Wagon fit that description. From the outside, it looked like a premium family hauler with a cargo area at the back.

MotorTrend kept one of the wagons in its long-term test fleet. When that test ended in 2011, the magazine named the car one of the greatest sleeper hot rods.

Showroom demand never matched that reception. The low production total is what places the wagon on MotorTrend's list of sales flops.

The Volkswagen Phaeton sold poorly when it was new and has since gained respect, according to MotorTrend. The outlet includes it on a list of 10 cars that flopped in showrooms but have aged well.

5 scary driving scenarios (and what to do)

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

From tire blowouts to flooded roads, learn what to do in five frightening driving emergencies, and how to stay in control when the unexpected happens

Driving can feel routine until something unexpected happens. A deer darts across the road, a tire blows out or another driver suddenly heads straight toward you. In those moments, panic can take over, making it harder to react safely.

The good news is that knowing what to do before trouble starts can make a frightening situation easier to manage. You can't control every driver, weather event or hazard on the road, but you can prepare yourself to respond calmly and make decisions that reduce the risk of a crash.

Reader’s Digest shared advice for handling some of the most unsettling situations drivers can face, from losing control on a slippery road to encountering dangerous weather. The common thread is simple: stay as calm as possible, avoid sudden movements and prioritize getting yourself and others out of danger.

Here are five scary driving scenarios and what to do if they happen to you.

A deer appearing in the road can trigger an instinctive reaction to jerk the steering wheel out of its way. Resist that urge. Swerving can send your vehicle into another lane, into oncoming traffic or off the road.

Instead, keep both hands firmly on the wheel and apply the brakes firmly. Reader’s Digest recommends maintaining control of the vehicle rather than making a sudden evasive turn. If you need to steer, use a controlled shuffle-steering motion to keep both hands engaged.

It’s a frightening few seconds, especially when an animal appears without warning, but a predictable response is safer than a sharp maneuver. Focus on keeping your vehicle under control and avoid creating a second hazard while trying to avoid the first.

A blowout can make the car pull sharply and leave you feeling as though you've lost control. The instinct may be to slam on the brakes, but that can make the situation worse.

Reader’s Digest recommends staying calm, easing off the accelerator and gradually reducing speed. You can brake lightly as needed, then steer toward a safe place off the road. Keep a firm grip on the steering wheel, particularly if a front tire blows, since the vehicle may pull in an unexpected direction.

Avoid jerking the wheel or making abrupt corrections. Once you've reached a safe spot, get out of traffic's way before dealing with the tire or calling for help.

A blowout is unsettling, but the goal isn't to stop immediately. It's to slow down in a controlled way and get the vehicle somewhere safe.

Seeing a vehicle heading directly into your lane is one of the most terrifying things that can happen behind the wheel. You may have only seconds to react, so avoid freezing or staring at the oncoming car.

Reader’s Digest recommends trying to get the other driver's attention by honking and flashing your headlights while looking for a way to avoid a direct collision. If you need to move, focus your eyes on the safest available path, whether that's an open lane or the shoulder.

Your vehicle tends to follow your line of sight, so fixating on the approaching car can make it harder to steer away from danger. Keep your attention on where you want to go and make the safest controlled move available.

There's no guaranteed way to avoid a collision in this situation, but staying focused on an escape route gives you a better chance of responding deliberately instead of panicking.

A road covered in water can be deceptive. What looks like a shallow puddle may conceal a much deeper stretch, and moving water can sweep a vehicle away.

Reader’s Digest advises against driving through water more than four inches deep. Even relatively shallow flooding can damage your engine and the electronic systems in your vehicle. Never attempt to drive through moving water, since the current can carry your car away.

If you can't tell how deep the water is, don't assume it's safe. The safest choice is to find another route rather than risk crossing. If you determine that a stretch is shallow enough to cross, the article recommends letting oncoming traffic pass first and proceeding slowly and steadily.

The important thing is not to let impatience or pressure from traffic convince you to take a risk. Turning around may cost you a few minutes; driving into floodwater can put your life in danger.

Losing traction on a slippery road can make it feel as though your car has a mind of its own. The key is to avoid overcorrecting and focus on the direction you want the vehicle to travel.

Reader’s Digest explains that when the rear of a car slides, drivers should look toward the path they want to take rather than at the obstacle they're trying to avoid. Your steering tends to follow your eyes, so focusing on a safe route can help you guide the car back under control.

That doesn't mean making a sudden, dramatic turn. Keep your movements controlled and give yourself room to correct the skid without jerking the wheel.

Winter driving calls for extra patience, too. Slippery roads make it harder to slow down and stop, so leave more distance between your vehicle and the one ahead. If conditions are especially poor, slowing down or finding a safe place to stop is better than pushing on.

Blueair InvisibleMist review: A humidifier without the usual mess?

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The Blueair InvisibleMist skips visible mist and white dust. Here's how it handles humidity, refills, cleaning, and everyday use

If you've ever run an ultrasonic humidifier, you probably know the cleanup routine: white dust on nearby furniture, damp spots around the machine, and another reminder to buy distilled water. The Blueair InvisibleMist takes a different approach. It uses evaporative technology to add moisture to the air without producing a visible cloud or scattering mineral dust around the room.

At $149.99, it's a relatively premium option for a compact humidifier. It has a 3.5-liter top-fill tank, automatic humidity control, app connectivity, and a washable tank. Blueair also advertises up to 48 hours of runtime, which sounds appealing if you'd rather not refill it every day.

The catch is that evaporative humidifiers work differently from mist-producing models, and independent testing found some limitations with runtime and cleaning. The InvisibleMist has plenty going for it, but whether it's worth the price depends on what you want from a humidifier.

The InvisibleMist is a compact evaporative humidifier designed for bedrooms, offices, and other medium-sized spaces. Its 3.5-liter tank and wick-based system work together to pull water through a filter and circulate air over it, adding moisture gradually rather than releasing droplets into the room.

That design is the main selling point. Minerals in tap water stay behind in the wick instead of becoming airborne, so you don't need to buy distilled water or wipe white residue off your furniture. The lack of visible mist also means fewer damp surfaces around the unit.

At 14.6 inches tall and 9.4 inches wide, the machine is small enough to fit on a dresser or side table. It includes three fan speeds, automatic humidity control, a built-in sensor, and a night mode. The Blueair app adds remote controls, scheduling, humidity settings, and filter-life tracking. The tank is dishwasher-safe, and the wick is designed to be replaced about every six months.

The InvisibleMist does a good job of bringing moisture into a dry room. Independent testing found that it could raise humidity from 30% to 60% in about 52 minutes, with a tested humidification capacity of 6 liters per day. That makes it a capable choice for a bedroom or medium-sized living space, even if it isn't built for very large rooms.

The evaporative system also has a natural limit: As the room gets more humid, the rate of evaporation slows. In one test, humidity rose quickly at first but leveled off a little above 50%. That's not necessarily a drawback. For most households, keeping humidity in a comfortable range is more useful than pushing it as high as possible.

Convenience is another strength. You can pour water directly into the top without removing the tank, and the automatic mode adjusts output based on the humidity sensor. The app is useful if you want to change settings from across the room or set a schedule. Night mode dims the display and keeps the fan running quietly.

The biggest drawback is the gap between the advertised runtime and what you may get in practice. Blueair promotes up to 48 hours on a fill, but independent testing found a much shorter runtime under its test conditions. Another evaluation measured just over 30 hours, while a separate lab test recorded 11 hours. Settings, airflow, and room conditions can make a significant difference, but don't assume you'll get two full days between refills.

Cleaning also takes more work than the dishwasher-safe tank might suggest. The tank is easy to access once removed, but getting to it means taking apart several pieces of the unit. Most components still need to be washed by hand, and the weekly cleaning routine can feel a little involved. The wick needs periodic replacement, too.

The controls are another minor frustration. The touchscreen is fairly dim and can be a little finicky, while the app offers useful basics rather than a particularly extensive set of smart features. And because this is an evaporative model, it won't deliver the same rapid humidity boost as some ultrasonic or warm-mist alternatives.

At $149.99, the InvisibleMist is also more expensive than many basic humidifiers. Its tap-water compatibility and low operating costs help justify the price over time, but shoppers who don't mind using distilled water may find cheaper options that offer longer runtimes.

The InvisibleMist makes the most sense for people who want a low-fuss way to manage dry indoor air without dealing with white dust or buying distilled water. Its compact size, quiet operation, automatic humidity control, and app features make it a good fit for bedrooms and home offices.

It's less appealing if you want a humidifier that can run for days without a refill or one that's especially easy to take apart and clean. People with very large rooms may also want a model with more capacity.

For everyday use in a medium-sized space, the Blueair offers a thoughtful alternative to traditional mist-producing humidifiers. Just go in knowing that the convenience of tap-water use comes with regular wick maintenance and more frequent refills than the advertised maximum runtime might suggest.
